var(--grid-column-gap));}}</style><div class="m_96bdd299 mantine-Grid-col __m__-_R_2muqssm_"><article class="blog-article-body"><address class="article-author visually-hidden">By <span><a rel="author" href="/blog/authors/threat-research-team">Threat Research Team</a></span></address><div style="--container-size:var(--container-size-xxl);max-width:calc(97.5rem * var(--mantine-scale));padding-inline:0rem" class="m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-size="xxl" data-strategy="block" id="post" data-component="post-content"><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Our threat hunters have been busy searching for abuse of the recently-released zero-day remote code execution bug in Microsoft Office (<code>CVE-2022-30190</code>). As part of their investigations, they found evidence of a threat actor hosting malicious payloads on what appears to be an <code>Australian VOIP telecommunications provider</code> with a presence in the <code>South Pacific nation of Palau</code>.</p><p>Further analysis indicated that targets in <code>Palau</code> were sent malicious documents that, when opened, exploited this vulnerability, causing victim computers to contact the provider’s website, download and execute the malware, and subsequently become infected.</p><p><strong>Key Observations</strong></p><p>This threat was a complex multi-stage operation utilizing <code>LOLBAS</code> (Living off the Land Binaries And Scripts), which allowed the attacker to initialize the attack using the <code>CVE-2022-30190</code> vulnerability within the <code>Microsoft Support Diagnostic Tool</code>. This vulnerability enables threat actors to run malicious code without the user downloading an executable to their machine which might be detected by endpoint detection.</p><p>Multiple stages of this malware were signed with a legitimate company certificate to add additional legitimacy and minimize the chance of detection.</p><p><strong>First stage</strong></p><p>The compromised website, as pictured in the screenshot below, was used to host <code>robots.txt</code> which is an executable which was disguised as “robots.txt”. We believe the name was used to conceal itself from detection if found in network logs. Using the Diagnostics Troubleshooting Wizard (<code>msdt.exe</code>), this file “robots.txt” was downloaded and saved as the file (<code>Sihost.exe</code>) and then executed.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2F102473DF-5366-4217-9D40-7123CFDED20A.png.webp%3Fitok%3D24HZ5fkS&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Second Stage, Sihost.exe</strong></p><p>When the renamed “robots.txt” – “Sihost.exe” – was executed by msdt.exe it downloaded the second stage of the attack which was a loader with the hash <code>b63fbf80351b3480c62a6a5158334ec8e91fecd057f6c19e4b4dd3febaa9d447</code>. This executable was then used to download and decrypt the third stage of the attack, an encrypted file stored as ‘<code>favicon.svg</code>’ on the same web server.</p><p><strong>Third stage, favicon.svg</strong></p><p>After this file has been decrypted, it is used to download the fourth stage of the attack from <code>palau.voipstelecom.com[.]au.</code> These files are named <code>Sevntx64.exe</code> and <code>Sevntx.lnk</code>, which are then executed on the victims’ machine.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" started with an innocent WhatsApp message.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003e“Hi David, I hope you are well. Are you at the office?”\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe sender claimed to be a real Gen executive based in Dublin. The profile used his name, photograph and an Irish telephone number. Nothing in the opening message mentioned money, urgency or an acquisition. It was simply designed to establish whether the recipient was available and willing to respond.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe recipient, whom we will call David, worked in Gen’s legal team and knew the colleague being impersonated. The unfamiliar telephone number raised suspicion, and the first phone conversation confirmed it: the caller’s voice did not match.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDavid knew he was dealing with a scam. Every subsequent message, call and document formed part of a controlled effort to understand how the operation worked and gather evidence about the people behind it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat followed involved a second impersonated identity, forged legal documents, a fictitious confidential acquisition and a request to transfer €626,735.45 to a company in Hong Kong.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat began as an attack against Gen’s legal team soon revealed a wider pattern. Our researchers found four additional targeted individuals who had received closely related NDA documents. The companies, advisers and acquisition narratives changed, but the structure, communication rules and document fingerprints remained remarkably consistent.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe call the campaign \u003cstrong\u003ePhantom Deal\u003c/strong\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eA real person, but neither his number nor his voice\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe first persona claimed to be someone from inside the company. The attacker had selected an identity that made sense in the context of Gen’s international operations and used a phone number with the correct country code to reinforce the illusion.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe deception did not survive the first call. David knew the person being impersonated, and the voice on the other end was not his.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter the initial exchange, a second person entered the conversation. This time, the attacker impersonated a genuine professional associated with PwC and asked the target to provide a private email address.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat request was not incidental.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe forged NDA that followed instructed the recipient to keep all communication related to the acquisition on WhatsApp and personal email. The attackers tried to keep the entire exchange outside corporate communication channels before introducing any payment request.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attackers had also studied Gen’s history. The acquisition narrative referenced Avast Software and NortonLifeLock Ireland Limited, drawing on the real acquisition of Avast by NortonLifeLock in 2022 and the subsequent creation of Gen Digital. The names were familiar, the corporate relationship had existed, and an intercompany transaction could appear plausible to someone operating under pressure and secrecy.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis was not a generic message sent to thousands of employees. The story had been adapted to its target. The targeting was tailored, but the execution was not flawless. David quickly identified inconsistencies in the explanation for why Avast should make a payment on behalf of NortonLifeLock Ireland Limited. His legal background and familiarity with internal transaction processes made those gaps easier to spot. A more coherent payment narrative could have made the same playbook considerably more convincing.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/1.png.webp?itok=5vk23yHy 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/1.png.webp?itok=E5i4m7y6 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1024,"height":768},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/1.png.webp?itok=B-ETEbcS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/1.png.webp?itok=1rKuJ4ot 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":668},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/1.png.webp?itok=VKH-EFVv 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/1.png.webp?itok=pkad8wX1 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":518}],"caption":"Figure 1. The initial WhatsApp approach used two impersonated identities. Names and identifying details have been changed or redacted.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/1.png.webp?itok=VKH-EFVv","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003eThe NDA was not supporting material\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe next stage was a professional-looking NDA branded as a document from PwC.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFake legal documents are common in corporate fraud, but the NDA served a more important role than simply making the acquisition story look credible. It established the rules under which the victim was expected to operate.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe document introduced a confidential acquisition, imposed a strict disclosure regime and set a near-term date for the supposed public announcement. Most importantly, it required communication to take place through WhatsApp and personal email.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eUnder normal circumstances, instructions to avoid company systems and exclude colleagues from a major transaction would be obvious warning signs. Inside the fiction created by the NDA, the same behaviour could be presented as a legal obligation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attackers were attempting to turn the company’s own confidentiality culture against it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe target was encouraged not to involve colleagues, not to discuss the transaction through normal channels and not to verify the instructions with Legal, Finance, Treasury, Compliance or Corporate Development. A recipient who treated the NDA as genuine would become increasingly isolated from the colleagues most likely to challenge the story.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe NDA was not an accessory to the attack. It was the payload.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/Diagram%20Light.png.webp?itok=T84OW-Wh 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/Diagram%20Light.png.webp?itok=jqFCzx2V 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":781},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/Diagram%20Light.png.webp?itok=RIJINQp3 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/Diagram%20Light.png.webp?itok=h1w_97Xb 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":594},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/Diagram%20Light.png.webp?itok=xFSEkIrH 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/Diagram%20Light.png.webp?itok=H0__5eAW 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":460}],"caption":"Figure 2. The NDA had been presented only days earlier.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat short window created pressure while providing a ready-made explanation for why the operation could not be discussed more broadly.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe supposed adviser then sent payment instructions asking Avast Software s.r.o. to transfer money on behalf of NortonLifeLock Ireland Limited.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe amount was precise: €626,735.45.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe beneficiary was a company in Hong Kong, with the payment described as an “Advance Retainer for Professional Services.” The document stated that the amount would be recorded as an intercompany receivable and reimbursed when the acquisition was formally announced.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt was a classic advance-payment fraud wrapped in the language of M\u0026amp;A, legal privilege and internal corporate accounting.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e“I need a SWIFT MT103”\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eOnce the payment instructions had been delivered, the tone changed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attacker began following up for confirmation that the transfer had been completed. The target was asked to provide a SWIFT MT103, the banking message used as evidence that an international transfer has been executed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003e“I need a swift MT103, it’s an official proof of wire transfer to attached to the package.”\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eLater, after receiving what appeared to be a confirmation email, the attacker complained that the link would not open and asked for the document as a PDF. He then added another requirement: the confirmation should contain the UETR number, a unique reference used to track a payment through the SWIFT network.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe request for the MT103 and UETR confirmed the objective. The attackers wanted proof that the funds were moving and the information needed to monitor the transfer, potentially reducing the time available for the company or its bank to intervene.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/2.png.webp?itok=c4RbyyJP 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/2.png.webp?itok=1rS-PiCd 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1024,"height":768},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/2.png.webp?itok=fxqzK_Yk 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/2.png.webp?itok=Z39SAK87 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":668},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/2.png.webp?itok=rSDP9_LT 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/2.png.webp?itok=sTeefbaj 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":518}],"caption":"Figure 3. The attacker repeatedly followed up for an MT103, then requested the UETR after the tracking link failed to open. Identity details have been redacted.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/2.png.webp?itok=rSDP9_LT","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003eTurning the attack into intelligence\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eOnce David had identified the scam, the objective shifted from verification to intelligence gathering. Working with our researchers, he continued the exchange while the team controlled the material being sent and monitored how the operation responded.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA fake account statement was prepared, showing what appeared to be the requested payment and the remaining company balance. The document contained a hidden marker that could identify whether it was opened on a system protected by one of our products.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the attackers requested formal MT103 confirmation, the team also created a fake Citibank-style confirmation email. It contained the transaction amount, beneficiary and payment reference expected by the criminals, together with a link to view the full transaction details.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe link did not lead to real banking information. It contained a canary token, allowing our researchers to record when it was opened.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen he reported that the link was not working, the team suggested that his VPN might be causing the problem and asked him to disable it. Further access attempts followed within minutes.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe token recorded 49 HTTP requests from 43 IP addresses over 24 days. Most of the activity in the first minutes came from automated scanners, cloud services and redirect-analysis systems, so the raw count does not represent 49 actions by the attacker.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter filtering that noise, the remaining data showed repeated interaction through VPNs, proxy services and several non-hosting internet connections. Some visits were separated by hours or days and used different browser profiles, behaviour consistent with a person returning to the link and attempting to retrieve the promised payment information.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe network data helped us separate scanner traffic, proxy infrastructure and repeated manual access, but it was not enough to attribute the operation. Some later visits came from ordinary ISP ranges, although those connections show where the traffic exited, not necessarily where the fraudsters were located.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe later visits showed repeated manual access by someone connected to the fraud operation, including long after the supposed transfer should have been completed.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-09/3.png.webp?itok=Ehkk4KTA 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-09/3.png.webp?itok=Je3qdknb 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1024,"height":768},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-09/3.png.webp?itok=1ataiWnB 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-09/3.png.webp?itok=EORM3ViU 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":668},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/3.png.webp?itok=hXXeoSeS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-09/3.png.webp?itok=xNY8mFa9 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":518}],"caption":"Figure 4. The controlled response included a fake account statement and a fake payment-confirmation email containing the tracked link. Sensitive banking and identity details have been redacted.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-09/3.png.webp?itok=hXXeoSeS","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003eFrom one attack to a wider campaign\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe controlled interaction gave us visibility into the live fraud attempt, while the forged NDA gave us something else: a set of document fingerprints we could hunt for.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eUsing its structure, wording and embedded identifiers, the team found four additional NDA samples connected to other targeted individuals.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe organizations and professions varied. The targets included senior people in private equity, industrial finance, sales, mining and energy. For each of them, an acquisition or strategic investment narrative would have been credible enough to justify initial engagement.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe advisers also changed. Some documents impersonated PwC, while others used KPMG or Ogier branding. There is no indication that any of these firms were compromised or involved in the operation. Their names and identities were being abused to make the documents appear legitimate.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDespite the different branding, the documents followed substantially the same sequence of sections and reused the same legal language. They all imposed confidentiality, directed communications towards WhatsApp and personal email, and introduced a short period between the NDA date and the supposed public announcement.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThey also contained the same unusual numeric identifier across documents attributed to different firms and prepared for unrelated recipients. This kind of residue is common when criminals reuse document templates. Names, dates, logos and transaction references may be changed, while less visible elements remain untouched.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe repeated structure suggests that the attackers were not building each operation from scratch. They had developed a reusable M\u0026amp;A fraud package that could be adapted to different people and companies.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn the Gen case, we observed the complete chain, from WhatsApp contact to payment request and demand for MT103 confirmation. For the other four targets, we found the malicious NDA documents carrying the same core narrative and document fingerprints, but did not observe the later payment stage.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTaken together, the evidence points to a wider M\u0026amp;A-themed fraud campaign built around the same narrative and document toolkit. It does not establish that every target received identical payment instructions or that every document was operated by the same individual.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eNo malware, no compromised mailbox\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ePhantom Deal\u003c/strong\u003e did not require an exploit, malicious attachment or stolen corporate email account.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe operation drew on publicly available information, real names, photographs, company history and familiar business procedures. WhatsApp made the contact immediate, the forged NDA justified secrecy, and the acquisition narrative created the urgency needed to move the victim towards payment. The financial request appeared only after the surrounding story had been established.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA security team searching only for malware or suspicious email links could miss the entire operation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe attack was designed to induce a process failure rather than exploit a technical vulnerability. The criminals attempted to convince the target that bypassing normal controls was necessary to protect a confidential deal.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe warning signs appeared well before the Hong Kong bank account. Keeping a sensitive corporate transaction on WhatsApp and personal email was already enough to require independent verification.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow companies can interrupt the chain\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eConfidential transactions require restricted access, but secrecy should never remove independent verification.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOrganizations can reduce exposure by establishing a small number of rules that apply even to the most sensitive acquisitions, investments and restructuring projects:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e79a03ebdcc50053e9752b64215ac6d0d\"\u003ePayment instructions must be verified through an independently established channel, not through contact details supplied during the transaction.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e56458281a15b902c959078fd579c2999\"\u003eNo executive, adviser or board member should be able to override payment controls through WhatsApp, personal email or a confidentiality claim.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e18c724391af3cb0258190c8ed3abd5c9\"\u003eLegal, Finance, Treasury and Corporate Development teams should have a defined escalation route for unexpected transaction requests.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e14239b646035ac07ac66d03bc0296926\"\u003eStaff involved in payments should be trained to recognize requests for MT103 documents, UETR references and other confirmation data as part of the fraud chain, not merely as administrative follow-up.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ea23d5cfe13818e8eefd6ca3cdb93d591\"\u003eExternal advisers should be contacted using verified directory information whenever their identity or instructions are in doubt.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli class=\"ck-list-marker-bold\" data-list-item-id=\"edfd65aefb0bda3b6fe5bc0523c729c49\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eChanges of communication channel, especially from corporate systems to private accounts, should trigger additional scrutiny.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe same rules must apply when the request appears to come from the CEO, a board member or someone already known to the recipient.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eAn NDA can limit who is told about a transaction. It should never prevent the transaction from being authenticated.\u003c/h3\u003e\u003ch2\u003eReal details, false transaction\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003ePhantom Deal\u003c/strong\u003e drew credibility from genuine information. The people, companies and advisers were real, as was the acquisition history connecting Gen and Avast. International payments, professional retainers and intercompany receivables are also normal elements of large corporate transactions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe transaction tying those details together was fabricated, and the execution was uneven. The caller’s voice did not match the colleague being impersonated, while the explanation for the payment contained inconsistencies that a lawyer familiar with the company’s processes could identify quickly. A more coherent story might have been considerably more dangerous.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhy try this against a security company? Probably for the same reason they try it anywhere else: a security company is still a company. It has money, confidential transactions and employees whose jobs do not involve analysing scams. The attackers were not trying to defeat our security technology. They were trying to find one person who might accept the story and step outside the normal process.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn this case, they chose someone who knew the colleague, recognized that the voice did not match and understood that the payment explanation made no sense. From that moment, the supposed victim was no longer following their script. He was helping us investigate it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is also some irony in how it ended. After spending days building a narrative around trust, secrecy and urgency, someone connected to the fraud operation repeatedly interacted with the fake payment confirmation we placed in front of them.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe NDA was intended to keep the operation confidential. Its reused language, structure and identifiers ultimately helped expose the campaign behind This is exactly the case with \u003ccode\u003eFF 25\u003c/code\u003e thunks, where WordlistLoader compares only the low 16 bits of the pointer, which the relocation delta cannot affect, as image bases are always aligned to 64 kB.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003ch3\u003eETW Bypass\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe second defense-evasion mechanism employed by WordlistLoader is a hardware-breakpoint-based ETW (Event Tracing for Windows) bypass, which comes down to setting a hardware breakpoint on \u003ccode\u003entdll!NtTraceEvent\u003c/code\u003e and registering a vectored exception handler. Every call to \u003ccode\u003eNtTraceEvent\u003c/code\u003e then raises an \u003ccode\u003eEXCEPTION_SINGLE_STEP\u003c/code\u003e exception, which the handler resolves by pointing the instruction pointer (\u003ccode\u003eEIP\u003c/code\u003e) at a stub that does nothing but return zero, and resuming execution there. The mapping from English words to byte values is defined by a build-specific wordlist of 256 distinct words: the word at index 0 decodes to \u003ccode\u003e0x00\u003c/code\u003e, the word at index 144 to \u003ccode\u003e0x90\u003c/code\u003e, and so on.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBoth the wordlist and the encoded sequence are stored as arrays of pointers, so the loader matches addresses rather than the strings themselves. Rebuilding the shellcode thus comes down to iterating over the word sequence, looking up each word in the list, and writing the index of the matching entry into the output buffer. Once the entire sequence has been processed, the buffer contains the reconstructed shellcode. Using the standard \u003ccode\u003ecall/pop\u003c/code\u003e trick, the stub takes its own return address as the patching target and enters an outer loop of 254 rounds, with each iteration first executing an inner delay loop and then patching the target address (which is the same for every iteration). The value written decreases with every round, ultimately reaching \u003ccode\u003e0x90\u003c/code\u003e on the final iteration. As shown in Figure 11 below, the patched byte is part of a \u003ccode\u003ecall dword ptr [eax-0x6F6F6F70]\u003c/code\u003e instruction, which corresponds to the \u003ccode\u003eFF 90 90 90 90 90\u003c/code\u003e opcodes. In version 4.0.2 Beta, all imports were resolved using a single hashing algorithm, so precomputing the hashes once was sufficient to annotate every call. In newer versions, the resolution is spread across dozens of nearly identical resolvers, each using its own multiply-rotate-XOR hash with different constants and operation order. Consequently, the same API name maps to a different hash value depending on which resolver handles the call. Hashes must therefore be precomputed separately for each resolver, slowing down the analysis.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFinally, as is quite typical for Amatera, it has revised its string obfuscation once again. Each string is now stored as a ciphertext blob, with its length and a numeric key index supplied at the decryption call site. For every byte, the key index and the byte's offset are mixed into a 64-bit state through four splitmix64-style rounds, producing four key bytes. The plaintext byte is then recovered as \u003ccode\u003eROR((ciphertext ^ key_3) - key_2, key_1) ^ key_0\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eInterestingly, the decryption routine also employs a deliberate stack modification aimed at thwarting decompilation. The modification occurs in a dead branch guarded by a flag that is never set anywhere in the binary. Should the branch ever be taken, it would subtract \u003ccode\u003e0x7FFFFF00\u003c/code\u003e from \u003ccode\u003eESP\u003c/code\u003e, call a stub that does nothing but return zero, and add the same value back to \u003ccode\u003eESP\u003c/code\u003e. Such a stack shift (of almost 2 GB) is nothing a regular compiler would ever emit, and is enough to break IDA's stack-frame reconstruction and make it refuse to decompile the function. For now, we have only observed this trick in a single function, but given the pace at which Amatera evolves, it would not be surprising to see it spread across the whole binary in the future.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_16.jpg.webp?itok=hAK4U6K4 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_16.jpg.webp?itok=MvJp_utg 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":507},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_16.jpg.webp?itok=cEooJJEG 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_16.jpg.webp?itok=HwVDJBXH 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":385},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_16.jpg.webp?itok=qmXz9Uu5 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_16.jpg.webp?itok=MSpVWJlT 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":299}],"caption":"Figure 16: Amatera’s stack manipulation.  ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_16.jpg.webp?itok=qmXz9Uu5","link":""}]},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003ch3\u003eHardened Syscall Invocation Through the WoW64 Transition\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFirst, it is appropriate to acknowledge \u003ca href=\"https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/wow64-subsystem-internals-and-hooking-techniques\"\u003eStephen Eckels\u003c/a\u003e, whose article \u003ca href=\"https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/wow64-subsystem-internals-and-hooking-techniques\"\u003eWOW64!Hooks: WOW64 Subsystem Internals and Hooking Techniques\u003c/a\u003e explains the mechanisms discussed below in greater detail. Second, it is worth highlighting that Amatera is a 32-bit stealer, as that is what makes the techniques described below possible.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn a 32-bit process running on 64-bit Windows, every interaction with the operating system has to eventually end in the 64-bit kernel. From the process's point of view, however, everything has to look as if it were running on 32-bit Windows. That is where the WoW64 (Windows on Windows) layer comes into play, seamlessly translating WinAPI calls from 32-bit user space to the 64-bit operating system kernel. Simply put, the whole mechanism is built around the fact that the process runs with two ntdlls loaded side by side: the 32-bit one the application interacts with, which \"forwards\" the call to the WoW64 layer, and the 64-bit one, used once the call reaches 64-bit mode.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn practice, the application calls a function in the 32-bit ntdll, which passes control to \u003ccode\u003entdll!Wow64SystemServiceCall\u003c/code\u003e. From there, the execution continues through \u003ccode\u003entdll!Wow64Transition\u003c/code\u003e and lands in \u003ccode\u003ewow64cpu!KiFastSystemCall\u003c/code\u003e, the stub through which the WoW64 layer leaves the 32-bit world. The transition is achieved by a far jump using the \u003ccode\u003e0x33\u003c/code\u003e selector, which switches the processor into 64-bit mode, where the WoW64 layer issues the actual syscall.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn an effort to be stealthier and evade user-mode hooks, Amatera skips the 32-bit ntdll part of that chain altogether, resolving the syscall numbers on its own and entering the WoW64 layer directly through \u003ccode\u003ewow64cpu!KiFastSystemCall\u003c/code\u003e. The transition itself is performed as an indirect call to \u003ccode\u003eTEB-\u0026gt;WOW32Reserved\u003c/code\u003e, a field that conveniently holds the address of \u003ccode\u003ewow64cpu!KiFastSystemCall\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn version 4.0.2 Beta, Amatera had a dedicated stub for every syscall it wanted to invoke this way, each of them loading the masked SSN from a global, unmasking it with a XOR, and calling \u003ccode\u003eTEB-\u0026gt;WOW32Reserved\u003c/code\u003e directly through the \u003ccode\u003ecall large dword ptr fs:0C0h\u003c/code\u003e instruction. However, regular applications do not make direct calls to \u003ccode\u003eTEB-\u0026gt;WOW32Reserved\u003c/code\u003e, making the sequence fairly noisy.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_17.jpg.webp?itok=LXPyWpod 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_17.jpg.webp?itok=sL2VsgNc 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":206},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_17.jpg.webp?itok=S_PqSahI 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_17.jpg.webp?itok=X0ZE0QwH 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":157},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_17.jpg.webp?itok=iElfyxz4 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_17.jpg.webp?itok=wt2O3GcZ 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":121}],"caption":"Figure 17: Example of a WoW64 syscall gate in Amatera 4.0.2 Beta. Reaching into a 64-bit process requires working with 64-bit pointers, which do not fit through the WoW64 gate, and while the 32-bit ntdll exposes a handful of \u003ccode\u003eNtWow64*\u003c/code\u003e helpers for exactly that purpose (such as, for example, \u003ccode\u003eNtWow64AllocateVirtualMemory64\u003c/code\u003e or \u003ccode\u003eNtWow64WriteVirtualMemory64\u003c/code\u003e), they do not cover everything Amatera requires for its injection technique.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThat is why Amatera employs Heaven's Gate, a technique that allows 64-bit code to be executed from within a 32-bit process on a 64-bit operating system (essentially, a manual version of the mode switch the WoW64 layer performs internally, naturally skipping the 32-bit ntdll layer as well). Heaven's Gate, as such, however, is nothing new in Amatera and has been there for quite some time already. What has changed is what the Gate leads to for a part of the calls Amatera makes through it.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn version 4.0.2 Beta, Amatera used Heaven's Gate to invoke 64-bit ntdll exported functions, passing the arguments according to the standard Microsoft x64 calling convention, with the execution therefore going through the export's prologue. However, just as AV and EDR products can hook exported functions in the 32-bit ntdll, they can hook the 64-bit ones as well. To avoid that, in newer versions, a fixed set of twelve syscalls (for example, \u003ccode\u003eNtReadVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eNtWriteVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eNtAllocateVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eNtMapViewOfSection\u003c/code\u003e, and \u003ccode\u003eNtSetIoCompletion\u003c/code\u003e) is set up to skip the export entirely, with Amatera resolving their syscall numbers on its own (and validating them against a pristine copy of ntdll read from disk) and issuing each call through an indirect x64 syscall trampoline built at runtime.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eEach trampoline consists of a 24-byte stub containing \u003ccode\u003emov r10, rcx\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003emov eax, SSN\u003c/code\u003e, and a RIP-relative indirect jump to a \u003ccode\u003esyscall; ret\u003c/code\u003e gadget inside ntdll (located by scanning the ntdll mapped in memory). To build and execute them, Amatera creates a section with \u003ccode\u003eNtCreateSection\u003c/code\u003e and maps it twice in its own process using \u003ccode\u003eNtMapViewOfSection\u003c/code\u003e: once with \u003ccode\u003eRW\u003c/code\u003e permissions for writing the stubs and once with \u003ccode\u003eRX\u003c/code\u003e permissions for running them.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_21.jpg.webp?itok=MXosRfAS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_21.jpg.webp?itok=6sRY9eYX 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":605},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_21.jpg.webp?itok=sJMtHBoM 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_21.jpg.webp?itok=dV79DGOG 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":460},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_21.jpg.webp?itok=gxquwPLu 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_21.jpg.webp?itok=lBYkFjGC 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":357}],"caption":"Figure 21: Amatera building the x64 syscall trampoline. In fact, the bypass is so alike that we believe Amatera's implementation was directly inspired by it. Therefore, as we have already covered the technique in \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/remus-64bit-variant-of-lumma-stealer\"\u003eour previous blog post\u003c/a\u003e, we will only do a quick recap of the bypass's main idea and focus solely on where Amatera's approach differs. For interested readers, we recommend revisiting our earlier post.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFrom a high-level perspective, the bypass relies on the fact that a running Chromium-based browser keeps the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e stored in memory. There, however, the key is protected with \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e using the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag, which limits decryption to the process that encrypted the data. Therefore, the bypass comes down to locating the protected key and injecting a payload that decrypts it using the complementary \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e from within the browser's context.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eTo find the key, Amatera, just like Remus and Lumma, scans the browser's memory for a 20-byte pattern, trying to find the \u003ccode\u003elea\u003c/code\u003e instruction that loads the address of the \u003ccode\u003eos_crypt_async::Encryptor\u003c/code\u003e vftable, as that is the class holding the protected \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e. On a match, it resolves the vftable address using the displacement of the \u003ccode\u003elea\u003c/code\u003e instruction, and since a pointer to it sits at the very beginning of every instance of that class, a second scan of the browser's memory makes it possible to locate a live instance holding the key. From there, extracting the protected \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e is just a matter of walking the object's tree of key entries and taking the one tagged \u003ccode\u003ev20\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe clearest evidence of the shared origin, however, is the signature itself. Amatera builds the pattern as \u003ccode\u003e48 8D 05 00 00 00 00 48 89 01 48 8B 02 48 89 41 00 48 8D 41\u003c/code\u003e and pairs it with the wildcard mask \u003ccode\u003e0xEFF87\u003c/code\u003e, the very same mask Remus applies to \u003ccode\u003e48 8D 05 8B CC 8D 02 48 89 01 48 8B 02 48 89 41 5B 48 8D 41\u003c/code\u003e. Breaking it down, the only positions in which the two patterns differ are the four displacement bytes of the \u003ccode\u003elea\u003c/code\u003e instruction and the displacement byte of the \u003ccode\u003emov\u003c/code\u003e instruction. These are precisely the five bytes the mask excludes from the comparison, which Amatera leaves zeroed out, while Remus keeps the values from whichever \u003ccode\u003echrome.dll\u003c/code\u003e build the pattern was lifted from. Everything else, the mask included, matches byte for byte.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=cTWIggL3 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=1R-iHwTw 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":511},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=enfvkIlA 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=Io9S_1U8 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":388},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=_qsKWD1x 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_22.jpg.webp?itok=jFUiFwqa 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":301}],"caption":"Figure 22: Amatera building the scan pattern. ","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-08/wordlistloader_amatera_figure_23.jpg.webp?itok=ddvxr1Ew","link":""}]},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003cp\u003eWhere Amatera's approach differs is in the size of the injected payload and the way it is injected into the browser. Remus and Lumma get by with less than a hundred bytes of shellcode as the payload has a single job: to call \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e on the memory pointing to the encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e and copy it into a pre-allocated buffer. Amatera's payload is considerably larger (thousands of bytes), but it also does considerably more. Besides the logic for decrypting the key, the payload carries the entire logic for locating it, as well as the logic for recovering the legacy \u003ccode\u003ev10_master_key\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eTo get the payload into the browser, Amatera creates two sections and maps each twice, once locally and once in the browser. The first section holds the payload, mapped locally as \u003ccode\u003eRW\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003eRX\u003c/code\u003e in the browser, whereas the second carries a configuration blob and the output area for the recovered keys, mapped as \u003ccode\u003eRW\u003c/code\u003e on both sides. The configuration blob holds the pointers to the native and crypto functions used by the payload, the path to the browser's \u003ccode\u003eLocal State\u003c/code\u003e, the address of the encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e, and the already mentioned ABE pattern with the mask should the payload need to locate the key again (by default, the key is located by the stealer itself, reading the browser's memory remotely, but the payload can repeat the search on its own as a fallback).\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFinally, Amatera's approach differs in the way the payload is launched. Rather than creating a remote thread, Amatera hijacks the browser's thread pool, using the technique known as PoolParty variant 7 (Remote \u003ccode\u003eTP_DIRECT\u003c/code\u003e Insertion), \u003ca href=\"https://www.safebreach.com/blog/process-injection-using-windows-thread-pools/\"\u003epublished by SafeBreach in 2023\u003c/a\u003e. It enumerates the browser's handles with \u003ccode\u003eNtQueryInformationProcess\u003c/code\u003e, duplicates them with \u003ccode\u003eNtDuplicateObject\u003c/code\u003e, identifies the I/O completion port among them with \u003ccode\u003eNtQueryObject\u003c/code\u003e, and queues a \u003ccode\u003eTP_DIRECT\u003c/code\u003e work item on it with \u003ccode\u003eNtSetIoCompletion\u003c/code\u003e, leaving the execution to one of the browser's own worker threads. The work item itself is a 72-byte \u003ccode\u003eTP_DIRECT\u003c/code\u003e structure assembled by hand, with the callback placed at a hardcoded offset, and written into a small remote allocation. Once the work item is queued, the stealer polls the output section for a marker signaling that the payload has finished.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003ch2\u003eSummary\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn this analysis, we dissected WordlistLoader, a new loader used to deliver Amatera through ClearFake campaigns. found a custom Windows backdoor on a single corporate workstation while hunting for unusual WMI persistence. The malware was small, had a limited command set and disguised itself as legitimate Realtek software. Its most unusual feature was its configuration: the address of its command-and-control server was not stored as readable text or encrypted data, but encoded in the number of spaces on each line of a Windows `desktop.ini` file. To a user, and to many automated inspection systems, the file would appear almost empty. To the malware, those spaces spelled out its server address. We found no evidence connecting the backdoor to a known threat actor, and the original infection happened before our protection was installed. However, the implant's custom design, its deployment on only one observed machine and the absence of related samples suggest that this may have been a deliberately targeted operation rather than a failed mass campaign.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003ch2\u003eAn event subscription named \"Realtek\"\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eMost malicious WMI subscriptions are easy to spot because the query asks for something no administrator would. one machine is the interesting part\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eEverything in this post came from a single host: a domain-joined Windows 7 SP1 workstation, 64-bit. One managed endpoint, and our prevalence figure for the binary is exactly one.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThat number is easy to misread, because low prevalence usually means a campaign that failed. Low prevalence should not mean low investigative priority: a threat sitting on one machine can matter more than one sitting on a hundred thousand, and a triage queue sorted by prevalence will show it to you last, if it shows it to you at all. The grounds for reading this one the other way need no victim detail at all.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe implant is custom-built: twelve kilobytes, no runtime library, and a config format that appears nowhere else in our corpus. None of the artefacts have ever been uploaded to VirusTotal in more than five years. We also searched VirusTotal by content for the fragments that would betray a sibling, including the \u003ccode\u003ec%s%se /c %s\u003c/code\u003e format string, the \u003ccode\u003ea=%s\u0026amp;r=none\u003c/code\u003e beacon body, \u003ccode\u003e/version/check.php\u003c/code\u003e inside Windows executables, and a stock UTF-16 \u003ccode\u003edesktop.ini\u003c/code\u003e header followed by runs of spaces. All four returned nothing. Two looser patterns, the table its checksum routine uses to rebuild a polynomial and a bare run of UTF-16 spaces, matched unrelated files and tell us nothing either way.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe domain also typosquats a Windows service, which is a choice made by someone who knew what \u003ccode\u003eDiagTrack\u003c/code\u003e was rather than the output of a generation algorithm, whatever VirusTotal's \u003ccode\u003edga\u003c/code\u003e tag suggests.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eA commodity loader that only ever lands on one machine is broken. A hand-written implant that only ever lands on one machine may be working exactly as intended.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWe will not push the assessment further than that. We never captured a task and never saw a second-stage payload, and we cannot tell you how the machine was reached, for a reason the timeline further down makes plain: the implant was already in place before our software arrived. Whatever the initial vector was, it happened on an endpoint nobody was watching. It imports 48 functions by name across \u003ccode\u003eKERNEL32\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eWININET\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eWS2_32\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eIPHLPAPI\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eSHLWAPI\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003eADVAPI32\u003c/code\u003e, and that is the whole dependency list. \u003ccode\u003emsvcrt.dll\u003c/code\u003e is not on it, and leaving it out took deliberate work. Grep the binary for it and you get nothing.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe size check is looser than it looks. The subtraction and the comparison are both unsigned — \u003ccode\u003ejbe\u003c/code\u003e in the disassembly — so it rejects only files of 174 to 223 bytes. \u003ccode\u003eOPEN_ALWAYS\u003c/code\u003e means a host with no config gets an empty \u003ccode\u003edesktop.ini\u003c/code\u003e created for it, \u003ccode\u003e0 - 174\u003c/code\u003e wraps to \u003ccode\u003e0xFFFFFF52\u003c/code\u003e, the guard passes, and the code asks the process heap for four gigabytes of zeroed memory. Nothing checks the result: on any machine that cannot satisfy that, the counting loop dereferences a null pointer. So on a host the operator never staged, the implant creates a zero-byte \u003ccode\u003eC:\\ProgramData\\desktop.ini\u003c/code\u003e and crashes on the spot.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThen it seeks to byte 174 and counts:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode\u003eSetFilePointer(hFile, 174, 0, FILE_BEGIN); // skip the stock Windows header ReadFile(hFile, payload, payload_size, \u0026amp;bytes_read, 0);\n\nline = 0; i = 0; do { if ( payload[i] == 13 ) // CR: next line, skip the LF { ++line; i += 2; } else { ++line_lengths[line]; // count one character on this line } i += 2; // stride 2: the file is UTF-16LE } while ( i \u0026lt; payload_size );\n\nlstrcpyA(g_server, line_lengths); // the counts are the string lstrcatA(g_server, \".com\");\n\n\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThere is no key and no decryption. \u003ccode\u003eline_lengths\u003c/code\u003e is an array of bytes holding one count per line, and the last two statements hand that array to \u003ccode\u003elstrcpyA\u003c/code\u003e as though it were a string. The number of characters on a line is the ASCII code of one character of the domain. This is unary encoding, the tally-marks of data formats.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe stride of 2 is there because the file is UTF-16LE, and the offset of 174 is not arbitrary: it is the exact byte length of a stock Windows \u003ccode\u003edesktop.ini\u003c/code\u003e, from the byte-order mark through a blank line, the \u003ccode\u003e[.ShellClassInfo]\u003c/code\u003e section header, \u003ccode\u003eLocalizedResourceName=@%SystemRoot%\\system32\\shell32.dll,-21781\u003c/code\u003e and its closing CRLF. The attacker invented no file format. Because each character costs as many UTF-16 spaces as its ASCII value, the config's size is a direct function of the domain it hides: \u003ccode\u003e174 + 2 * sum(character codes) + 4 * lines\u003c/code\u003e, which for \u003ccode\u003ediagrtrack\u003c/code\u003e gives 2,318 bytes exactly. The file size is a fingerprint of the domain, and the domain can be narrowed down from the file size alone.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003ch2\u003eThe C2 protocol\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eEach victim gets an eight-character identifier: the CRC-32 of \u003ccode\u003eUSERNAME\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eUSERDOMAIN\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003eCOMPUTERNAME\u003c/code\u003e concatenated, formatted as \u003ccode\u003e%08X\u003c/code\u003e. The same value becomes the name of a mutex, and a second copy waits a second for it before giving up. That matters more than it sounds, because the first copy never gives up: the binary has exactly one \u003ccode\u003eExitProcess\u003c/code\u003e call, in the configuration routine, reached only when the mutex is already held or the config is unusable. Past that point the process loops forever. The 19:50 trigger therefore fires to useful effect once per boot, and every later firing dies on the mutex.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe CRC is standard, but neither the polynomial nor the usual 256-entry lookup table is anywhere in the file. What is there is a 14-byte table of bit positions that the code walks at runtime, setting one bit per entry to rebuild \u003ccode\u003e0xEDB88320\u003c/code\u003e. That reads like an attempt to leave no constant for a scanner to key on, and it is not: it is zlib's \u003ccode\u003eDYNAMIC_CRC_TABLE\u003c/code\u003e build option, copied in whole. The appendix works out which decade the copy came from.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eEvery cycle then opens with an ICMP knock rather than an HTTP request. The backdoor resolves the domain, puts its eight-byte identifier in the payload of a 32-byte echo request, and waits a second for a reply. Only if the ping succeeds does it speak HTTP:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode\u003ePOST /version/check.php HTTP/1.1 User-Agent: Mozilla/5.0 (Windows NT 10.0; Win64; x64) AppleWebKit/537.36 (KHTML, like Gecko) Chrome/78.0.3904.108 Safari/537.36 Content-Type: application/x-www-form-urlencoded\n\na=\u0026lt;id\u0026gt;\u0026amp;r=none\n\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eA beacon with nothing to report sends \u003ccode\u003er=none\u003c/code\u003e: the first one, and every cycle where the last response carried no command or only a \u003ccode\u003etime\u003c/code\u003e change. Otherwise \u003ccode\u003er\u003c/code\u003e carries the previous command's output, base64-encoded and then URL-encoded. The user agent is a hardcoded Chrome 78 string, frozen at whatever was current when the author wrote it.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eTasking comes back in the response body, which the malware scans for the literal \u003ccode\u003eCMD=\u003c/code\u003e. There are three verbs:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\u003ccode\u003esystem\u003c/code\u003e runs a command through \u003ccode\u003ecmd.exe\u003c/code\u003e, captures stdout and stderr through a pipe, and gives up after 30 seconds with the text \u003ccode\u003ecmd timeout\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\u003ccode\u003eput\u003c/code\u003e writes a file, taking a path and base64 content\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\u003ccode\u003etime\u003c/code\u003e changes the polling interval, which starts at five seconds\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThat is the whole command set. No file listing, no screenshots, no keylogging. It base64-decodes the content, decrements the first byte and increments the second, writes the result to \u003ccode\u003etempcache.tmp\u003c/code\u003e, renames that to the target path, and only then reopens the file to patch the correct two bytes back. A dropped executable therefore sits on disk with \u003ccode\u003eL[\u003c/code\u003e where its \u003ccode\u003eMZ\u003c/code\u003e signature belongs, right up until a final two-byte write. The binary was compiled on 2 December 2020 at 13:34 UTC, and the config file on the victim's disk was created a little over three hours later. Build and deploy inside one afternoon.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eOur software was installed twenty-one days later. Every date that follows is from the middle of the incident rather than the start, and the first evening the backdoor started on that machine is not something we have any record of.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe last DNS record we hold for \u003ccode\u003ediagrtrack.com\u003c/code\u003e is from March 2021. The registration lapsed that July and nobody renewed it. Nobody re-registered it either: the domain has no whois record today and does not resolve. Even the squatters passed.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe backdoor did not notice. We collected the same WMI subscription from that host three times, in February, August and October 2021, which is how we know the persistence stayed in place rather than being cleaned and reinstalled. When a detection we wrote for this file shape went live in June 2022, it fired on the same machine, and the config was still sitting in \u003ccode\u003eC:\\ProgramData\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFrom the code we know what that machine did for the eleven months after the registration lapsed. The failure changed less than you would think, because the address buffer is never initialised and nothing checks the return value: every five seconds the implant read whatever bytes happened to be on its stack, called \u003ccode\u003einet_addr\u003c/code\u003e on them, and sent an ICMP echo to the result. There was no one on the other end for any of it.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003ch2\u003eDetection opportunities\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eConfiguration formats do not have to look like configuration formats. A routine that counts spaces is still a parser, and a file containing no suspicious strings, high-entropy blob or encoded text can still carry a C2 address in plain sight. Any pipeline that finds data mainly by looking for things that resemble data risks treating whitespace as empty.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eStructural detections are more durable. A hash covers one binary and a domain block covers one address, but a rule describing the shape of this configuration survives changes to both.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003ePast byte 174, a legitimate \u003ccode\u003edesktop.ini\u003c/code\u003e should not consist entirely of UTF-16 spaces, carriage returns and line feeds. We deployed a detection for that structure in June 2022 and ran it until November. Across our user base, it fired only on the machine we already knew about. It would nevertheless detect another configuration using the same technique with a completely different domain.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe same characteristics also provide several inexpensive hunting opportunities:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eLook for \u003ccode\u003e__EventFilter\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003eCommandLineEventConsumer\u003c/code\u003e pairs bound to \u003ccode\u003eWin32_LocalTime\u003c/code\u003e, particularly those launching executables from paths intended to resemble legitimate software.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eInspect \u003ccode\u003eC:\\ProgramData\\desktop.ini\u003c/code\u003e, especially when it contains the Program Files localization string \u003ccode\u003eshell32.dll,-21781\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eTreat a zero-byte \u003ccode\u003eC:\\ProgramData\\desktop.ini\u003c/code\u003e as potentially significant. That does not tell us how consequential the intrusion was, because we never captured a command, recovered a second-stage payload or established how the machine was first compromised. It does tell us that prevalence alone would have been a poor reason to ignore it.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe implant was custom-built, its configuration format was absent from the rest of our corpus, and searches for related binaries and protocol fragments found no convincing siblings. We assess with moderate confidence that it was deployed selectively, while stopping short of naming an actor or campaign.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBy the time our detection found it, the C2 domain had been expired for eleven months. The backdoor was still starting at 19:50 after every reboot, rebuilding the address from lines of spaces, and trying to contact an operator who was no longer there.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe detection was not wrong. It was late.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003ch2\u003eAppendix: build and CRC provenance\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eTwo details that change nothing about the story but pin down what the author built this with.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003eThe missing runtime\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003emsvcrt.dll\u003c/code\u003e is absent from the import table, and that absence took work. \u003ccode\u003eSHLWAPI\u003c/code\u003e is there to supply \u003ccode\u003ewnsprintfA\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eStrStrA\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003eStrToIntA\u003c/code\u003e in place of \u003ccode\u003esprintf\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003estrstr\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003eatoi\u003c/code\u003e, the \u003ccode\u003estr*\u003c/code\u003e family is \u003ccode\u003elstrcpyA\u003c/code\u003e and friends from \u003ccode\u003eKERNEL32\u003c/code\u003e, and \u003ccode\u003ememset\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003ememcpy\u003c/code\u003e are the author's own two routines, because the compiler emits calls to those whether you link a runtime or not. The binary also carries its own compiler receipt in \u003ccode\u003e.rdata\u003c/code\u003e:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode\u003eGCC: (tdm64-1) 4.9.2\n\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThat is TDM-GCC, a MinGW-w64 distribution, and the linker version of 2.24 and the missing Rich header agree with the string. The PE timestamp reads 2 December 2020, 13:34 UTC. A default build from that toolchain would enter at \u003ccode\u003emainCRTStartup\u003c/code\u003e and import \u003ccode\u003emsvcrt.dll\u003c/code\u003e, so the runtime had to be left out on purpose and the entry point aimed at the author's own function.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003eThe polynomial that is not there\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe 14-byte table in \u003ccode\u003e.rdata\u003c/code\u003e is a list of bit positions:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode\u003e0, 1, 2, 4, 5, 7, 8, 10, 11, 12, 16, 22, 23, 26\n\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAt runtime the code walks that table and sets one bit per entry, counting down from bit 31:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cpre\u003e\u003ccode\u003efor ( i = 0; i != 14; ++i )\n    poly |= 1 \u0026lt;\u0026lt; (31 - tap_table[i]);\n\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/pre\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe result is \u003ccode\u003e0xEDB88320\u003c/code\u003e, the reflected CRC-32 polynomial, which has exactly 14 bits set. Before crediting the author for that: this is zlib. The routine is \u003ccode\u003ecrc32()\u003c/code\u003e line for line, from the null-pointer guard through the pre- and post-inversion to the eight-bytes-at-a-time unrolled loop, compiled with \u003ccode\u003eDYNAMIC_CRC_TABLE\u003c/code\u003e — the zlib build option that generates the lookup table on first use instead of shipping a kilobyte of constants — and GCC has inlined the static \u003ccode\u003emake_crc_table\u003c/code\u003e into it, which is why the table build sits inside the same function. zlib built the polynomial from that list of exponents from January 1996 until the 1.2.12 release, in March 2022, replaced it with a plain \u003ccode\u003e#define POLY 0xedb88320\u003c/code\u003e. The comment sitting directly above the list writes the same polynomial out longhand, as \u003ccode\u003ex^32+x^26+x^23+x^22+x^16+x^12+x^11+x^10+x^8+x^7+x^5+x^4+x^2+x+1\u003c/code\u003e, so the table is the polynomial as a mathematician would state it rather than a constant kept out of a scanner's way. It did not go away for tidiness either: the commit that removed it rewrote the whole routine around the interleaved CRC method of Kadatch and Jenkins, and the new \u003ccode\u003e#define\u003c/code\u003e was a byproduct of chasing a factor of three in speed.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe confirmation is in \u003ccode\u003e.data\u003c/code\u003e, which in this binary holds exactly one initialised variable: a \u003ccode\u003edd 1\u003c/code\u003e that the CRC routine clears once the table exists. That is zlib's \u003ccode\u003ecrc_table_empty\u003c/code\u003e. What is not there is the thread-safety flag zlib added in September 2004, so their copy of \u003ccode\u003ecrc32.c\u003c/code\u003e came from some zlib released between 1996 and 2004, at least sixteen years stale by the time they compiled it. The missing constant is a build option, not first URL was already dead.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIt did not load a credential form or show a fake Microsoft login page. Instead, it returned an expired tracking response, which is exactly what the operator wanted scanners and analysts to see after the first click.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat could easily have been the end of the investigation: one reported phishing email, a burned link and no visible payload. But the expired URL still exposed enough of the underlying infrastructure to start pulling the thread. What followed led us to Mailer-Go Mission Control, a bulk phishing platform we had not seen publicly documented at the time of analysis.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMailer-Go handled the delivery side of the campaign, including email templates, recipient management, SMTP infrastructure, proxy support and per-recipient tracking links. The final phishing stage came later, through a Cloudflare Workers page running EvilTokens, a known Microsoft OAuth Device Code phishing kit.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEvilTokens itself was not the new part. What caught our attention was the machinery around it: how victims were routed there, how the operator switched infrastructure when a domain was burned, and how much of the delivery chain was designed to disappear once a useful click had already happened.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe link was expired, but the pattern was not\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe reported email contained a tracking URL under:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ecoldchain-distribution[.]com\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe URL followed a structure we later associated with Mailer-Go:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehxxps://\u0026lt;8-hex\u0026gt;.link.\u0026lt;domain\u0026gt;/t/v3.\u0026lt;campaign-id\u0026gt;.\u0026lt;recipient-token\u0026gt;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach recipient received a unique token. Once that token was used, later requests no longer reached the live phishing chain and instead returned an expired response. A victim could therefore see the real destination, while a scanner or analyst arriving afterwards saw something very different.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat behavior became one of the more useful clues in the investigation. The link was not simply a route to a phishing page. It also acted as a gate that closed after use, limiting what could be recovered later from the same URL.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe first domain, coldchain-distribution[.]com, was registered on 2026-06-01. Once detections started catching up, the operator moved activity to healthprofessionals21[.]org, which had been registered the same day. Its tracking subdomains resolved to the same Linode IP used by the earlier campaign, and more than 40 *.link.healthprofessionals21[.]org subdomains were eventually observed.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eA fake blog on the front door\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAt the root, the replacement domain looked harmless.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ehealthprofessionals21[.]org served a technology and gaming blog with articles about consoles, VPNs and multimedia devices. The content was thin and generic, but plausible enough to give scanners and casual visitors something ordinary to index.
